# Korean 01 coverage matrix

## Stage purpose

- Locale and variety: `ko-KR`, contemporary standard South Korean.
- Default productive register: ordinary polite `해요체`.
- Canonical learner input: standard Hangul spelling and spacing only. No Latin transliteration or phonetic respelling is permitted.
- Planned size: exactly 1,000 original cards, `ko-01-0001` through `ko-01-1000`.
- Structure: twenty-five binding slices of exactly 40 cards. Slices are fresh generation and review units; their usable Korean is cumulative under the prerequisite graph.
- Entry point: complete Korean 00 or equivalent direct Hangul reading, typing, sound, connected-delivery, phrase-boundary, and essential-expression control.
- Outcome: the complete basic operational system needed for ordinary daily life and the TOPIK 1 range, balanced across listening and production.
- Boundary: Korean 00 is an immutable prerequisite and duplicate-comparison inventory. It is never a sentence template, paraphrase source, or source of Korean 01 primary targets.

Completion is decided by the inventories, contrasts, and acceptance checks below, not by reaching the last ID alone. Every card has one focused, normalized-unique primary target. A tense change, number substitution, counter substitution, name change, or larger hidden span does not create independent coverage by itself.

## 1. Grammar and constructions

- Productive present copula `이에요/예요` and negative copula `아니에요` in identification and correction contexts.
- `있어요/없어요` for existence, static location, possession, and availability, with the intended function recoverable from the situation.
- Ordinary descriptive predicates in present and past polite forms.
- Basic noun and adjective modification only inside independently useful, lexicalized chunks; no productive noun-modifier paradigm.
- Core particle control: `은/는`, `이/가`, `을/를`, `에`, `에서`, `도`, `만`, `와/과`, `하고`, limited spoken `(이)랑`, natural `의`, `부터/까지`, limited recipient `에게/한테`, and limited direction or means `으로/로`.
- Natural Korean information order and omission of recoverable subjects or objects.
- Basic deictic control for `이/그/저` before nouns, `이것/그것/저것`, and `여기/거기/저기`, always anchored to the speaker, listener, prior discourse, or visible scene.
- Ordinary polite present and past, and expected or planned future `-(으)ㄹ 거예요`.
- Short negation `안`, inability or failed performance `못`, existential negative, and negative copula, each kept distinct.
- High-frequency `하다` constructions and only those irregular or contracted forms that occur naturally in accepted beginner vocabulary.
- Noun request `주세요`, action request `-아/어 주세요`, polite instruction or invitation `-(으)세요`, and desire `-고 싶어요`.
- Short-clause coordination or sequence `-고`, ordinary cause or sequence `-아서/어서`, and basic contrast `-지만`.
- A small connector set: `그리고`, `그래서`, and `그런데` in short operational discourse.
- Productive beginner subject honorification `-시-`, plus a small essential lexical set, always supported by a recoverable relationship and subject.
- Questions about people, objects, places, time, reason, manner, choice, quantity, price, and availability, with `?` retained when it is needed for an unambiguous audio cue.

## 2. Communicative functions

- Introduce oneself; exchange name, nationality, language, occupation, workplace, and study information.
- Identify and describe family members, ordinary relationships, age, and possession.
- State where people and objects are; describe rooms, household objects, and availability.
- Ask and answer practical questions about identity, objects, places, schedules, quantity, price, choice, and availability.
- Tell dates and time; arrange appointments; state frequency, duration, opening times, and ordinary schedules.
- Describe daily routines and recurring activities at home, school, and work.
- Order food and drinks; state preferences; request items or service; handle simple restaurant choices.
- Shop for quantities, sizes, colors, clothing, and everyday goods; ask prices and complete simple payment interactions.
- Use basic transport, destinations, stations, route numbers, direction, means, and travel-time information.
- Handle classroom tasks, workplace schedules, telephone numbers, simple messages, and contact information.
- Describe weather, season, physical condition, and immediate non-specialist health needs.
- Invite, choose, request, express desire, give simple directions or instructions, and respond politely.
- Describe simple past events and state expected or planned future actions.
- Link two short clauses by coordination, sequence, reason, or contrast.
- Clarify, confirm, correct, request repetition, and repair a routine service interaction.
- Select ordinary or subject-honorific wording for teachers, older family members, customers, and routine service contacts.

## 3. Core lexical domains

- Identity, countries, nationalities, languages, occupations, workplaces, courses, and study.
- Family, household members, friends, classmates, colleagues, teachers, customers, and ordinary titles.
- Homes, rooms, furniture, appliances, belongings, possession, and static location, including the basic spatial relations beside and between.
- Daily routines, chores, recurring actions, hobbies, and frequency.
- Calendar, weekdays, dates, clock time, appointments, duration, and schedules.
- Food, drinks, meals, menus, restaurant service, tastes, and preferences.
- Shops, products, quantities, colors, sizes, clothing, money, prices, payment, and stock.
- Streets, stations, stops, vehicles, tickets, destinations, route information, and travel time.
- Schools, classrooms, materials, lessons, assignments, workplaces, and basic communication.
- Weather, seasons, temperature, simple outings, and expected plans.
- Body, ordinary symptoms, rest, food or drink needs, and non-specialist help.
- Telephone digits, messages, contact details, floors, and room numbers where they solve a common daily task.
- Invitations, choices, plans, past events, clarification, confirmation, and service repair.

The domain lists are planning pools, not accepted lexical indexes. An item counts only after it occurs naturally in an accepted card and is entered in the accepted lexical/chunk index.

## 4. Collocations and chunks

Planning favors reusable collocation families rather than one card per headword. Required families include:

- stating a nationality, speaking a language, studying a subject, attending a school, and working at a place;
- introducing a family member, stating an age, living with someone, and having or not having a possession;
- being in a room, placing an object at a location, doing an action at a location, and going to a destination;
- waking up, eating a meal, leaving home, taking transport, arriving, studying or working, returning, and resting;
- setting a date, telling clock time, meeting from one time to another, and stating a weekly frequency or duration;
- ordering an item, asking for an action, choosing a serving, stating a preference, and asking whether an item is available;
- asking a price, selecting color or size, counting goods, paying, and requesting ordinary packaging or checking;
- taking a bus or train, getting on or off, going by a means, asking a route number, and stating travel time;
- reading, writing, listening, speaking, asking a question, answering, sending a simple message, and checking contact details;
- describing weather or condition, stating a basic symptom or need, and choosing a near-term plan;
- linking two useful actions, giving a concrete reason, contrasting two options, and repairing or confirming information.

These are semantic and collocational families, not preapproved cloze strings. Generation must not turn a family into a mechanical noun, number, or tense series. Korean 00 strings may appear only as already-known support when natural; they cannot be copied, lightly expanded, or paraphrased into a new primary target.

## 5. Pronunciation and orthography

- Reuse all Korean 00 letter, block, keyboard, simple 받침, covered complex 받침, connected-sound, phrase-grouping, and final-intonation control.
- Revisit plain, aspirated, and tense consonants inside accepted high-frequency words and collocations, never as isolated sound labels.
- Select major vowel spellings from the known word and situation. No audio-only spelling contrast is allowed where contemporary pronunciations overlap. In particular, retain possessive particle `의` in writing when its ordinary connected-speech realization varies.
- Preserve standard underlying spelling through phrase-final neutralization, resyllabification, nasalization, tensification, aspiration involving `ㅎ`, and `ㄴ/ㄹ` lateralization.
- Hear and produce ordinary contractions in accepted speech, including the high-frequency `해요`, `돼요`, `봐요`, `줘요`, and `마셔요` families, while typing their standard spellings.
- Keep particles attached to the preceding word; keep counters and bound units spaced as required; preserve the spaces in `-고 싶어요`, `-(으)ㄹ 거예요`, `안` or `못` negation, and `-아/어 주세요`; attach `-(으)세요` to the verb.
- Give dates, clock time, prices, telephone digits, age, and counters ordinary phrase grouping. Sound changes at number-counter boundaries never license a changed spelling.
- Retain `?` for questions and invitations when punctuation is needed to cue the complete audio. Terminal punctuation remains outside the typed answer.
- Use normal careful beginner speech and common connected delivery. No pronunciation score, sound-only answer, or alternate phonetic spelling is accepted.

### Recurring sound-and-spelling assignments

| Context | Binding treatment | Slices |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Plain, aspirated, and tense onsets | Recover the intended known word from the full situation; preserve lexical spelling. | 1–25, concentrated in 2, 7–9, 13–16, 25 |
| Major simple and compound vowels | Use lexical and sentence context, not an unreliable sound-only distinction; recover written `의` from its possessive function even when ordinary connected delivery varies. | 1–25, concentrated in 1–4, 11, 13, 15 |
| Simple and common complex 받침 | Type the accepted word's standard final and retain it through inflection. | 1–25, concentrated in 3–4, 7, 13, 16–19 |
| Resyllabification | Recognize a final before a vowel-initial particle or ending without moving the written consonant. | 1, 3–5, 7, 9, 11–25 |
| Nasalization | Recognize common final-plus-nasal contexts in accepted daily vocabulary; retain both underlying spellings. | 3–4, 7–8, 11–16, 19, 24–25 |
| Tensification | Recognize common post-final and compound contexts, including number-counter phrasing where natural; never insert an unlicensed doubled letter. | 4–16, 19–25 |
| Aspiration involving `ㅎ` | Recognize both `ㅎ`-before-consonant and consonant-before-`ㅎ` contexts in accepted words and inflections. | 7–9, 15–18, 20–25 |
| `ㄴ/ㄹ` lateralization | Recognize it in accepted place, transport, schedule, and service vocabulary while retaining spelling. | 5, 8, 10, 13, 18, 24–25 |
| Contractions and reductions | Hear ordinary contracted polite forms and type the standard contracted form selected by the accepted vocabulary. | 2, 7, 9, 11–25 |
| Phrase grouping and final contour | Distinguish statement, information question, confirmation, invitation, request, and instruction using wording, situation, punctuation, and contour together. | 1–25 |
| Spacing | Particles attach; counters separate; request, desire, future, and negation forms follow standard spacing. | 1–25, explicitly audited in every slice |

## 6. Register

- Ordinary polite `해요체` is the productive default with unfamiliar adults, staff, teachers, adult classmates or colleagues, and routine service contacts.
- A learner refers to self with `저` where an explicit subject is useful. Repeated subject pronouns are omitted when context already identifies the participant.
- Names, kinship terms, and titles are preferred over unnecessary second- or third-person pronouns. `당신` is not taught as a neutral everyday equivalent of “you.”
- `이름+씨` is limited to ordinary address or reference among adults of similar status; it is not used downward toward a child or upward toward a teacher or supervisor.
- `선생님`, `손님`, family terms, and role titles occur only in settings where the address is natural.
- `-시-` honors the grammatical subject. It is not inserted merely because the listener is older, and it does not honor the speaker's own action.
- The fixed conventional formal-ending expressions already accepted in Korean 00 remain whole inherited chunks. They do not license productive `합니다체` conversion in Korean 01.
- No casual intimate endings, broad workplace honorifics, humble-verb system, stylized speech, slang, dialect, or strongly gendered production is introduced.

## 7. Pragmatics

- Use `은/는` for established topic or contrast and `이/가` for identification, existence, and focused or newly supplied information; do not translate either as one English word.
- Distinguish existence, location, possession, and availability through the whole scene rather than treating `있어요/없어요` as a single undifferentiated pair.
- Use `에` for a destination, time, or static location and `에서` for an action location or source. The chosen predicate and event determine the particle.
- Omit recoverable subjects and objects; do not copy English pronoun frequency or word order.
- Keep refusal, factual correction, inability, lack, and prohibition separate. Korean 00 prohibition or permission chunks may support a situation but do not open a productive permission system.
- Make noun requests, action requests, instructions, invitations, and subject-honorific statements distinguishable by the object, actor, relationship, and situation.
- Use `-시-` only for an appropriate respected subject, including a teacher, older family member, customer, or service addressee whose action is the subject of the clause.
- Keep invitations non-presumptive, requests courteous, corrections calm, and service interactions concise.
- Preserve natural titles and kinship address. Avoid an explicit pronoun when the relationship already identifies the participant.
- Keep connected discourse to one or two short clauses. A connector card must have a practical reason for linking the clauses.

## 8. Listening targets

- Recover one focused hidden span from a complete, fair, natural Korean sentence.
- Distinguish `이에요/예요`, `아니에요`, and the four operational uses of `있어요/없어요` from context.
- Hear topic, subject, object, destination/time/location, action-location/source, inclusion, restriction, coordination, recipient, and means/direction particles in short speech.
- Distinguish present, past, and expected future; `안` and `못`; descriptive and action predicates; and ordinary versus subject-honorific forms.
- Distinguish noun requests, action requests, polite instructions or invitations, and desire.
- Follow number system and counter selection in dates, clock time, prices, age, telephone numbers, quantities, floors, rooms, routes, frequency, and duration.
- Recognize standard words through resyllabification, nasalization, tensification, `ㅎ` aspiration, lateralization, and ordinary contractions.
- Use phrase boundaries and final contour to distinguish statements, questions, confirmations, invitations, requests, and instructions.
- Follow two short clauses linked by coordination, sequence, reason, or contrast without requiring connected narration.
- Track a recoverable omitted subject across one short operational exchange.

## 9. Production targets

- Type one focused answer in standard Hangul spelling and spacing with no transliteration support.
- Select copula, existential form, particle, tense, polarity, request form, connective, or honorific form from the situation.
- Produce useful beginner words and collocations in complete daily-life contexts rather than isolated dictionary lists.
- State and ask about personal information, family, home, routine, food, shopping, transport, school, work, schedules, weather, health, and plans.
- Produce native- and Sino-Korean numbers with the correct beginner function, counter, spacing, and standard spelling.
- Produce present, past, and expected future, plus short negation, inability, desire, requests, instructions, and short clause links.
- Maintain ordinary polite `해요체`; use subject honorification only with a justified subject and relationship.
- Type standard underlying forms despite connected pronunciation and preserve required punctuation outside the answer.
- Repair, clarify, confirm, and respond politely in a short service or classroom interaction.
- Complete cumulative TOPIK 1 listening-to-typing tasks without relying on a Korean 00 sentence pattern.

## 10. Prerequisites

- Stage entry requires all ten Korean 00 slices or equivalent control. This includes direct Hangul input, the complete modern letter inventory, covered 받침, common connected-speech behavior, standard-spelling recovery, phrase grouping, final intonation, and the forty accepted survival expressions.
- The complete Korean 00 source inventory is `cards/ko-KR/00.json`. All 300 accepted `answer` values, including short syllable or block targets, are protected against Korean 01 normalized duplication.
- Korean 00 fixed expressions may occur only as already-known support. They are not productive grammar evidence and cannot be copied, expanded mechanically, or paraphrased into Korean 01 targets.
- Within Korean 01, the binding graph is cumulative and linear:

```text
Slice 1 → Slice 2 → Slice 3 → Slice 4 → Slice 5
        → Slice 6 → Slice 7 → Slice 8 → Slice 9 → Slice 10
        → Slice 11 → Slice 12 → Slice 13 → Slice 14 → Slice 15
        → Slice 16 → Slice 17 → Slice 18 → Slice 19 → Slice 20
        → Slice 21 → Slice 22 → Slice 23 → Slice 24 → Slice 25
```

- A slice may use only Korean 00 and accepted material from its declared earlier Korean 01 slices. A planned item is not usable support until its card has passed fresh Korean-editor review and all acceptance checks.

## 11. Exclusions

- Productive ability `-(으)ㄹ 수 있다/없다`, obligation `-아/어야 하다`, conditions `-(으)면`, intentions `-(으)려고 하다`, attempts `-아/어 보다`, and experience `-아/어 본 적이 있다`.
- Systematic before/after constructions, systematic comparison or limit constructions, present/past/future noun-modifying clauses, indirect quotation, and connected multi-event narration.
- Productive formal polite `합니다체`, expanded honorific or humble systems, passive or causative systems, and broad business language.
- Abstract argument, institutional written language, specialized medicine, Hanja study, dialect production, slang, aggressive language, and strongly gendered or character-like speech.
- Latin transliteration, phonetic respelling, a Korean `reading` field, isolated sound labels, audio-only ambiguous spelling, and pronunciation scoring.
- Mechanical tense, polarity, name, noun, number, price, date, or counter substitution as separate breadth.
- Reuse, light expansion, or paraphrase of a Korean 00 primary target; hiding a larger arbitrary span to conceal duplication.
- Long complete-sentence clozes except a compact fixed high-value expression; a cloze crossing `.`, `?`, `!`, or a speaker-turn boundary; punctuation inside `cloze` or `answer`.
- Prompt storage, review transcripts, review state, a content database, a morphology or number engine, ASR, forced alignment, committed audio, archives, images, or unrelated infrastructure.

## Binding grammar and contrast inventory

### Construction assignments

| Construction or function | Binding treatment | Closest contrast or boundary | Slice(s) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `이에요/예요` | Productive identification after consonant- and vowel-final nouns in complete situations. | `아니에요`; existence with `있어요`. | 1–2, 25 |
| Negative copula `아니에요` | Reject or correct a noun identity. | `없어요` for lack/nonexistence; `안` predicate negation. | 1–2, 19, 25 |
| `있어요/없어요`: existence | Introduce whether a person or thing exists. | Possession, location, and availability readings. | 4, 14, 25 |
| `있어요/없어요`: location | State where a known person or thing is or is not. | Existence; action location with `에서`. | 4, 8, 25 |
| `있어요/없어요`: possession | State whether a person has something. | Existential scene without a possessor. | 3, 10, 25 |
| `있어요/없어요`: availability | Ask or state whether a product, seat, time, or service is available. | General existence or possession. | 11–13, 25 |
| Descriptive predicates | Ordinary present and past state descriptions in useful domains. | Action predicates; copular noun descriptions. | 11, 13, 15–17, 25 |
| Lexicalized noun/adjective modification | Only accepted fixed collocations such as everyday labels and product descriptions; no form-building lesson. | Productive noun-modifying clauses are excluded. | 3, 13, 15, 25 |
| `은/는` | Established topic and explicit contrast. | `이/가` for identification, focus, existence, or new information. | 1–4, 7, 11, 13, 19, 25 |
| `이/가` | Identification, focused answer, new information, and existential subject. | `은/는`; object `을/를`. | 1–4, 11, 15–19, 24–25 |
| `을/를` | Mark the affected object of an ordinary action. | Subject `이/가`; omission of a recoverable object. | 7, 9–13, 17–23, 25 |
| `에`: destination | Endpoint of motion. | Source/action location `에서`; direction/means `으로/로`. | 8, 18, 21–22, 25 |
| `에`: time | Appointment or event time. | Range `부터/까지`; duration without `에`. | 5–7, 10, 17–18, 25 |
| `에`: static location | Location with existence or state predicates. | Action location `에서`. | 4, 8, 24–25 |
| `에서`: action location | Place where an action occurs. | Static `에`. | 7–13, 17, 21–23, 25 |
| `에서`: source | Place from which motion or contact begins. | Destination `에`; range start `부터`. | 8, 10, 18, 21–22, 25 |
| `도` | Addition or shared applicability. | Restrictive `만`; neutral topic/object marking. | 2, 7, 11, 13, 23, 25 |
| `만` | Restrict a choice or quantity in a practical scene. | Additive `도`; no systematic limit construction. | 2, 6, 11–14, 23, 25 |
| `와/과` | Written-neutral noun coordination in short useful groups. | Spoken-frequent `하고`; clause linkage `-고`. | 2–3, 13–14, 25 |
| `하고` | Common spoken noun coordination or accompaniment. | `와/과`; recipient `한테`; clause `-고`. | 2–3, 7, 11, 18, 25 |
| `(이)랑` | Limited common spoken coordination or accompaniment in otherwise ordinary polite speech. | `하고` and `와/과`; do not treat it as a casual speech-level ending. | 2–3, 11, 18, 25 |
| `의` | Possession or affiliation only where natural and not normally omitted; ordinary spoken variation never changes the written particle. | Juxtaposition, compound, or contextual omission. | 2–3, 10, 25 |
| `부터/까지` | Basic time or place range with a concrete start and endpoint. | Time/destination `에`; source `에서`. | 5–6, 8, 10, 25 |
| `에게/한테` | High-frequency recipient contexts; `한테` in ordinary speech and `에게` in neutral contexts. | Accompaniment `하고`; source `에서`. | 10, 20–22, 25 |
| `으로/로` | Basic direction or means, with correct choice after final `ㄹ`. | Destination `에`; action location `에서`. | 8, 10, 18, 21, 25 |
| Basic deictic series | Productive `이/그/저` before nouns, `이것/그것/저것`, and `여기/거기/저기` in visible or discourse-anchored scenes. | Near speaker, near listener/previously mentioned, and distant from both; question choices with `어느/어디`. | 4, 8, 13, 25 |
| Argument omission and word order | Omit recoverable people or objects and keep time-place-manner order natural. | Unnecessary pronoun repetition or copied English order. | 1–25 |
| Information and choice questions | Productive beginner `누구`, `뭐`, `어디`, `언제`, `왜`, `어떻게`, `어느`, `무슨`, `몇`, `얼마` in justified scenes. | Confirmation questions and statements. | 1–15, 18–20, 25 |
| Ordinary polite present | Current state, routine, or immediate action. | Past and expected future. | 1–25 |
| Ordinary polite past | Completed event or past state, including natural contracted and irregular forms. | Present routine/state; expected future. | 17, 19, 21–25 |
| `-(으)ㄹ 거예요` | Expected or planned future based on a concrete schedule or intention. | Present schedule and past event; no `-(으)려고 하다`. | 18, 23–25 |
| Short negation `안` | Chosen nonperformance or ordinary predicate negation. | Inability/failure `못`; negative copula; `없어요`. | 19, 23, 25 |
| `못` | Inability or failed performance in a concrete situation. | Volitional `안`; no ability paradigm. | 19, 22–23, 25 |
| High-frequency `하다` | Productive noun-plus-`하다` collocations in present, past, negated, and linked forms. | Do not treat every noun as licensing a new verb. | 2, 7, 9–10, 17–23, 25 |
| Noun `주세요` | Request a named item or quantity. | Action request `-아/어 주세요`. | 11–14, 20, 25 |
| `-아/어 주세요` | Ask the listener to perform a concrete action. | Noun `주세요`; instruction/invitation `-(으)세요`. | 9–12, 20, 25 |
| `-(으)세요` | Ordinary polite instruction, invitation, or service direction with the addressee as actor. | Action request; third-person subject-honorific statement/question. | 12, 20, 24–25 |
| `-고 싶어요` | Speaker desire or a contextually appropriate question about the listener's desire. | Expected future plan; no intention or attempt construction. | 18, 23, 25 |
| `-고` | Coordinate predicates or give a short action sequence. | Noun coordination; causal/sequence `-아서/어서`; contrast `-지만`. | 21, 23, 25 |
| `-아서/어서` | Give an ordinary cause/reason or a natural tightly linked sequence. | Neutral `-고`; contrast `-지만`. | 22–23, 25 |
| `-지만` | Contrast two short states, actions, or options. | Additive `-고`; causal `-아서/어서`. | 23, 25 |
| Sentence connectors | `그리고`, `그래서`, `그런데` across at most two short sentences or turns. | Clause endings above; no long narration. | 21–23, 25 |
| Productive subject `-시-` | Present, past, and expected-future forms with a respected subject and recoverable relationship. | Ordinary subject form; addressee-directed `-(으)세요`. | 24–25 |
| Essential honorific lexemes | `계시다`, `드시다`, `주무시다`, `성함`, `연세`, and `말씀` only in ordinary beginner contexts; do not add a second `-시-` to a lexical verb that already contains it. | Ordinary counterparts; no broad conversion or humble system. | 24–25 |
| High-frequency irregulars and contractions | `하다`, `되다`, common `ㅂ`, `ㄷ`, `르`, `ㅡ`, `ㅅ`, `ㅎ`, and `ㄹ`-before-`시` behavior only where accepted vocabulary makes them useful. | No exhaustive class table or rare-item padding. | 2, 7–9, 13, 15–20, 22, 24–25 |
| Inherited permission/prohibition chunks | Korean 00 cards `0062` and `0099` may support a situation as fixed wholes but never become Korean 01 primary targets. | No productive permission, prohibition, or ability system. | 12, 19–20, 25 |
| Korean 00 conventional formal chunks | Retain Korean 00 cards `0004`, `0033`, `0181`, `0183`, `0193`, `0194`, and `0291` as unanalyzed inherited support. | Productive Korean 01 output remains `해요체`; no formal-style conversion. | 25 |

### High-frequency form inventory

This is a binding pattern inventory, not a license to create conjugation tables. Each row needs several natural accepted words or collocations from its assigned domain, and every resulting primary target must still pass the Korean 00 and cumulative Korean 01 duplicate checks.

| Form family | Natural operational domain | Required control | Slice(s) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `하다` and `되다` contractions | Study, work, routine, service, readiness, and change of state | Present, motivated past, negation, request/connective forms where natural; standard contracted spelling. | 2, 7, 9–10, 17–23, 25 |
| `ㅂ` irregular | Weather, temperature-related condition, description, and difficulty | High-frequency present and past forms that arise naturally; no rare descriptive list. | 13, 15–18, 22–25 |
| `ㄷ` irregular | Listening, walking, and routine movement | Present, past, and connective forms only in accepted high-frequency vocabulary. | 7–9, 17, 19, 21–25 |
| `르` irregular | Knowledge, speed, selection, and immediate condition | Natural polite and connective forms; Korean 00 repair language remains support rather than a new target. | 8, 13, 16–19, 22–25 |
| `ㅡ` loss | Writing, size, busyness, pain, and other accepted daily predicates | Natural polite present/past and request/connective forms with standard spelling. | 7, 9, 13, 16–17, 20–25 |
| High-frequency `ㅅ` irregular | Improvement or recovery in a basic health context | Require at least one common operational recovery form in accepted vocabulary; do not add rare predicates to expand the pattern. | 16–17, 22, 25 |
| High-frequency `ㅎ` irregular | Color, condition, and ordinary condition questions | Natural accepted descriptive forms only; do not make a color-paradigm series. | 13, 15, 17, 23, 25 |
| `ㄹ` behavior before `-시-` | Knowing, living, opening, or another accepted high-frequency honorific context | Contrast the standard ordinary stem with the naturally inflected subject-honorific form. | 20, 24–25 |
| Copula consonant/vowel selection | Identity, occupation, dates, and labels | Choose `이에요/예요` from the preceding noun; contrast negative copula. | 1–2, 5, 25 |

### High-risk contrast map

| Required contrast | Situational distinction that must be visible or audible | Introduction and cumulative audit |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `은/는` versus `이/가` | Established topic/contrast versus identity, focus, new information, or existential subject; no one-word translation rule. | 1, 3–4; revisit 7, 11, 13, 19; final 25 |
| `에` versus `에서` | Static location, destination, or event time versus action location or source; predicate/event controls the choice. | 4, 7–8; revisit 10, 17–18, 21–22; final 25 |
| Four uses of `있어요/없어요` | Existence has no possessor; possession has a possessor; location has a known entity and place; availability has a requested resource, time, seat, or product. | 3–4, 11–13; consolidated 14; final 25 |
| Present versus past versus expected future | Routine/current state, completed event/past state, and scheduled/expected action must differ in time reference and situation, not only ending. | present 1–16; past 17; future 18; mixed 21–25 |
| `안` versus `못` | Choice or ordinary negation versus inability or failed performance; neither is replaced by the ability construction reserved for later. | 19; causal/contrast revisit 22–23; final 25 |
| `주세요`, `-아/어 주세요`, `-(으)세요` | Requested item/quantity, requested listener action, and polite instruction/invitation/service direction have distinct actors and speech acts. | 9, 11–12; systematic 20; honorific disambiguation 24; final 25 |
| `-고`, `-아서/어서`, `-지만` | Addition/sequence, cause or tightly linked sequence, and contrast must be motivated by the event relation. | 21, 22, 23; final 25 |
| Ordinary subject form versus `-시-` | Honorification follows the grammatical subject and relationship, not the listener's age alone; self-honorification is rejected. | 24; final 25 |
| `-(으)세요` versus subject-honorific statement/question | An addressee-directed instruction or invitation contrasts with a statement/question about a recoverable respected subject, even when surface forms resemble each other. | 20, 24; final 25 |
| Ordinary `해요체` versus inherited conventional formal chunks | Fixed Korean 00 items remain complete formulas; Korean 01 does not derive new formal equivalents or mix styles randomly. | final 25 |

## Binding number and counter inventory

### Number-system scope

| System or form | Binding scope | Spelling, spacing, and pronunciation requirement | Real-world functions | Slices |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Native Korean cardinals | Productive `하나` through `아흔아홉` only where a counter, age, hour, frequency, or duration makes the value useful. | Use counter forms `한`, `두`, `세`, `네`, and exact-twenty `스무` before the appropriate counter; compound values retain the appropriate tens form; keep the counter spaced and retain standard spelling through boundary sound changes. | People, objects, animals, books, sheets, drinks, vehicles, clothing, bowl and meal counts, age, hours, occurrences, and durations. | 3–6, 8–14, 24–25 |
| Sino-Korean cardinals | Productive decimal composition needed for dates, minutes, prices, telephone digits, floors, rooms, routes, restaurant portions, temperatures, measurements, and formal/documentary age; larger values occur only in a real task. | Group a multi-digit quantity by its real function rather than arbitrary substitution; keep units spaced according to standard usage; standard spelling remains unchanged in connected speech. | Calendar, money, restaurant portions, contact details, identifiers, measurements, and service information. | 5–6, 8, 10, 12–15, 24–25 |
| Zero forms | `공` for telephone and identifier digits; `영` for zero as a value such as temperature. | Select by function; do not offer them as interchangeable accepted answers. | Telephone numbers, room or route identifiers, temperature. | 10, 15, 25 |
| Native counter contractions | `한`, `두`, `세`, `네`, `스무` as required before counters. | The written contraction is part of the answer when the counter context requires it; no phonetic alternative is accepted. | All native-counter interactions. | 3–6, 8–14, 24–25 |
| Short day-duration forms | The lexical native forms `하루`, `이틀`, `사흘`, and `나흘` in common short-duration contexts; longer numeric durations may use Sino-Korean `일`. | Write each lexical form as one word; never substitute a mechanically composed form such as `하나 일`. | Short trips, closures, visits, and periods of illness or rest. | 5, 14, 16–18, 25 |
| Decimal place values | Tens, hundreds, thousands, and ten-thousands only inside genuine prices, years, room/route/contact identifiers, and measurements. | Avoid card series made by changing one digit; require natural phrase grouping and exact standard spacing. | Prices, years, contact or service details. | 5, 10, 13–14, 25 |

All learner-produced number targets are written as Korean number words in Hangul. Arabic digits may supply visible real-world context only when they are outside the cloze and cannot substitute for the required Hangul production.

### Functions, counters, and units

| Function / counter or unit | Required number system | Spacing and pronunciation focus | Required real-world use | Slices |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| General objects `개` | Native | Counter form and one space, including `한/두/세/네/스무`; ordinary boundary tensification where it occurs does not alter spelling. | Household objects, products, and order quantities. | 4, 11, 13–14, 25 |
| People `명` | Native | Counter form plus one space; distinguish from respectful `분`. | Family/group size, classmates, reservations. | 3, 12, 14, 25 |
| Respectful people `분` | Native | Counter form plus one space; distinguish native person count from Sino minute count by context. | Customers, teachers, or older guests. | 24–25 |
| Animals `마리` | Native | Counter form plus one space; preserve 받침 and phrase grouping. | Pets and common animals. | 3, 14, 25 |
| Books `권` | Native | Counter form plus one space; standard spelling across the number-counter boundary. | Classroom, library, or purchase quantities. | 9, 14, 25 |
| Flat items `장` | Native | Counter form plus one space. | Paper, tickets, cards, and flat goods. | 8–9, 13–14, 25 |
| Bottles `병` | Native | Counter form plus one space; distinguish the plain written onset from connected delivery. | Drink or product quantities. | 11–14, 25 |
| Cups/glasses `잔` | Native | Counter form plus one space. | Café, home, and restaurant drink orders. | 11–12, 14, 25 |
| Machines/vehicles `대` | Native | Counter form plus one space; lexical context resolves vowel spelling. | Cars, buses, taxis, computers, or appliances. | 8, 10, 14, 25 |
| Clothing `벌` | Native | Counter form plus one space. | Clothing purchase and possession. | 13–14, 25 |
| Pairs `켤레` | Native | Counter form plus one space; preserve the accepted lexical spelling. | Shoes or socks. | 13–14, 25 |
| Servings `인분` | Sino | Number plus one space; keep the whole counter as one word. | Restaurant portion count and shared dishes. | 12, 14, 25 |
| Bowls/servings `그릇` | Native | Counter form plus one space; preserve the complex final. | Soup, rice, or noodle servings where natural. | 11–12, 14, 25 |
| Meals `끼` | Native | Use `한/두/세 끼` with one space and preserve the counter's written tense onset. | Number of meals in a daily routine, meal plan, or basic health discussion. | 6–7, 11, 14, 16, 25 |
| Ordinary age `살` | Native | Counter form plus one space; use `스무 살`, not the uncontracted form before the counter. | Personal or family age in ordinary polite conversation. | 3, 14, 25 |
| Formal or documentary age `세` | Sino | Number plus one space; keep it distinct from the native everyday `살` pattern. `세` is not honorific by itself. | Forms, notices, announcements, and other formal numeric-age contexts. | 24–25 |
| Clock hour `시` | Native | Counter form plus one space; preserve spelling despite natural boundary tensification. | Appointments, opening time, departures, routines. | 5–6, 8, 10, 12, 18, 25 |
| Clock minute `분` | Sino | Number plus one space; context distinguishes minutes from respectful people. | Exact times and travel time. | 5–6, 8, 10, 18, 25 |
| Half hour `반` | Fixed time quantity | One space after the hour expression; natural phrase grouping. | Common appointment and schedule time. | 6, 10, 18, 25 |
| Year `년`, month `월`, calendar day `일` | Sino | Standard spacing in complete dates; explicitly retain the standard month forms `유월` and `시월`; group dates as one calendar unit. | Birth year where appropriate, current year, appointments, travel, school schedule. | 5, 10, 17–18, 24–25 |
| Weekday `요일` | Named weekday system | Write the weekday as one word; distinguish weekday from numeric date. | Schedules, opening days, invitations. | 5–6, 10, 18, 25 |
| Currency `원` | Sino | Number and `원` are spaced; group thousands and ten-thousands naturally; no digit-substitution series. | Asking, stating, checking, and paying a price. | 13–14, 25 |
| Telephone digits | Sino digits, with `공` for zero | Speak digit by digit in meaningful groups; write the accepted number format consistently; no arbitrary number padding. | Exchanging, confirming, and correcting contact information. | 10, 19, 25 |
| Occurrence/frequency `번` | Native | Counter form plus one space. | Weekly frequency, attempts at contact, or repeated routine events without teaching attempt grammar. | 6–7, 10, 14, 25 |
| Route/identifier `번` | Sino | Number plus one space; context must distinguish an identifier from occurrence count. | Bus route, queue, item, or contact identifier. | 8, 10, 14, 25 |
| Ordinal `번째` | Native | Use `첫 번째` for first and the appropriate native form from second onward, with one space before `번째`. | Floor-independent sequence, classroom choice, or queue position. | 9–10, 14, 25 |
| Floor `층` and room `호` | Sino | Number plus one space; ordinary connected delivery with standard spelling. | Finding a broadly useful building floor or room. | 8, 10, 13–14, 25 |
| Duration `시간` | Native | Counter form plus one space; contrast a duration with clock `시`. | Study, work, sleep, or travel duration. | 6–8, 14, 17–18, 25 |
| Duration `분` | Sino | Number plus one space; contrast duration with an exact clock minute through context. | Waiting, walking, transit, or break duration. | 6, 8, 14, 19, 25 |
| Numeric duration `일` | Sino | Number plus one space in a numeric duration expression; context distinguishes duration from calendar date and from the lexical short-day forms `하루/이틀/사흘/나흘`. | A dated notice, reservation, service period, or other context where a numeric day count is natural. | 5, 14, 16–17, 25 |
| Duration `주` and `달` | Native | Counter form plus one space. | Course, visit, plan, or recurring schedule. | 5–6, 14, 18, 25 |
| Duration `개월` | Sino | Number plus one space; use only where a service or course duration makes it natural. | Course, rental, or appointment interval. | 10, 14, 25 |
| Temperature `도` | Sino, with `영` for zero | Number plus one space; standard spelling through connected delivery. | Weather and seasonal plans. | 15, 18, 25 |
| Distance `미터`, `킬로미터` | Sino | Number plus one space; keep the Hangul unit spelling and group the measurement as one phrase. | Walking directions, station access, and ordinary travel distance. | 8, 14, 25 |
| Weight `그램`, `킬로그램` | Sino | Number plus one space; use natural market quantities rather than a conversion drill. | Food and everyday shopping. | 13–14, 25 |

Every number card must add a distinct function, counter choice, spelling or pronunciation issue, or interactional move. Merely replacing one numeral with another is rejected as mechanical duplication.

## Lexical and accepted-target inventories

### Korean 00 audit baseline

The complete 300-card `cards/ko-KR/00.json` inventory was audited before this plan. Its accepted primary comparison key is every card's `answer`, normalized as described below. The baseline includes whole expressions, words, phrases, syllables, and single-block targets. It remains external to this file and must be loaded afresh for every generation and review session; copying it into the Korean 01 ledger would falsely make prerequisite material look like new coverage.

### Normalization and duplicate decisions

For each candidate card:

1. Normalize `answer` to Unicode NFC, trim leading and trailing whitespace, and collapse every internal whitespace run to one ordinary space. Terminal punctuation is not part of an answer under the card contract.
2. Reject an exact or normalized match against all 300 Korean 00 answers and every accepted Korean 01 primary target.
3. Build a semantic signature from the canonical lemma or fixed chunk, sense, construction, communicative function, and argument pattern. Compare it against Korean 00 and all accepted Korean 01 cards; reject a semantic duplicate even when inflection, particle, word order, or a larger hidden span differs.
4. Compare the slice batch for mechanical series: tense-only, polarity-only, name-only, noun-only, digit-only, price-only, date-only, or counter-only changes do not establish distinct breadth without a distinct function or contrast assignment.
5. Do not rescue a duplicate by hiding an arbitrary larger part of the same sentence.
6. Add rows to both indexes only after the independent Korean-editor review, source-contract audit, and duplicate audit accept the final card. Rejected and draft cards never enter either index.

### Accepted primary-target index format

This index starts empty. It is populated cumulatively, one row per accepted card and no earlier. `normalized_primary_target` is the unique comparison key; `semantic_signature` documents why a superficially different target is or is not independent.

```text
card_id | normalized_primary_target | target_kind | canonical_lemma_or_fixed_chunk | construction_or_function | semantic_signature | slice | accepted_review
```

### Deduplicated lexical/chunk index format

This separate index also starts empty. It counts useful lexical breadth only after accepted usage and merges inflections and mechanical variants. A polysemous form receives separate rows only when the accepted cards establish genuinely distinct operational senses.

```text
canonical_entry | entry_kind | operational_sense_or_function | first_accepted_card_id | accepted_card_ids | attested_forms | slices | evidence_count
```

- `entry_kind` is `lemma`, `compound`, `collocation`, or `fixed_chunk`.
- Inflected forms of one lemma share a row unless they establish a lexicalized distinct sense.
- Particle swaps, tense changes, polarity changes, counter values, and number values do not make new lexical rows.
- A multiword chunk is indexed only when it is independently reusable and occurs naturally in an accepted card; an arbitrary cloze span is not a chunk.
- `accepted_card_ids`, `attested_forms`, and `slices` are deduplicated sets maintained after acceptance.
- Every counted entry must point to at least one accepted Korean 01 card. Planned vocabulary and rejected drafts are never counted.
